    "license": "http://creativecommons.org/licenses/by/4.0/","articleSection": "Research Article","name": "Haunted Histories of Experimental Psychology: A Re-Examination of Hodgson and Davey\u2019s Mal-Observation Report","headline": "Haunted Histories of Experimental Psychology: A Re-Examination of Hodgson and Davey\u2019s Mal-Observation Report","abstract": "In 1887, psychical researchers Richard Hodgson and S. J. Davey published a study that revealed counterintuitive aspects of human cognition: How healthy, sober, honest observers of events can be prone to seemingly remarkable errors of eyewitness testimony. Their report, titled \u2018The Possibilities of Mal-Observation and Lapse of Memory from a Practical Point of View\u2019 included a collection of eyewitness statements from participants in a series of hoax séances. Hodgson and Davey asserted that all phenomena in their séances were produced using techniques drawn from performance magic. However, their participants were not initially informed about this fact. In their accounts, participants consistently attributed events that they saw, not to trickery, but to spiritual phenomena and psychic powers on the part of Davey, who played the role of the \u2018medium\u2019. A major advantage this novel paradigm was that the experimenters had a highly reliable record of actual events, because they had orchestrated the séances themselves. Their paper emphasised the contrasts between what their participants reported remembering relative to what had actually occurred. The errors of the witnesses were marked by failures to notice or remember key aspects of the séance, and even instances where the participants seemed to confabulate- confidently reporting events that had never really taken place. Neither Hodgson nor Davey would have identified themselves as psychologists or cognitive scientists, rather they considered themselves to be psychical researchers, operating under the auspices of the Society for Psychical Research (SPR). At the time, experimental psychology was only just beginning to emerge as a formal and distinct academic discipline. But looking back on their work from a modern perspective, it arguably anticipated more formal theoretical and empirical developments in cognitive psychology by decades. Ironically, given that their report systematically documented failures of attention and lapses of memory, it has been largely ignored in historical considerations of psychology research and is relatively unknown today. In this paper, I will explore the historical context of Hodgson and Davey\u2019s report and discuss how some of their key findings can be related to our contemporary scientific understanding of attention, memory, metacognition, and the science of magic.",
    "description": "In 1887, psychical researchers Richard Hodgson and S. J. Davey published a study that revealed counterintuitive aspects of human cognition: How healthy, sober, honest observers of events can be prone to seemingly remarkable errors of eyewitness testimony. Their report, titled \u2018The Possibilities of Mal-Observation and Lapse of Memory from a Practical Point of View\u2019 included a collection of eyewitness statements from participants in a series of hoax séances. Hodgson and Davey asserted that all phenomena in their séances were produced using techniques drawn from performance magic. However, their participants were not initially informed about this fact. In their accounts, participants consistently attributed events that they saw, not to trickery, but to spiritual phenomena and psychic powers on the part of Davey, who played the role of the \u2018medium\u2019. A major advantage this novel paradigm was that the experimenters had a highly reliable record of actual events, because they had orchestrated the séances themselves. Their paper emphasised the contrasts between what their participants reported remembering relative to what had actually occurred. The errors of the witnesses were marked by failures to notice or remember key aspects of the séance, and even instances where the participants seemed to confabulate- confidently reporting events that had never really taken place. Neither Hodgson nor Davey would have identified themselves as psychologists or cognitive scientists, rather they considered themselves to be psychical researchers, operating under the auspices of the Society for Psychical Research (SPR). At the time, experimental psychology was only just beginning to emerge as a formal and distinct academic discipline. But looking back on their work from a modern perspective, it arguably anticipated more formal theoretical and empirical developments in cognitive psychology by decades. Ironically, given that their report systematically documented failures of attention and lapses of memory, it has been largely ignored in historical considerations of psychology research and is relatively unknown today. In this paper, I will explore the historical context of Hodgson and Davey\u2019s report and discuss how some of their key findings can be related to our contemporary scientific understanding of attention, memory, metacognition, and the science of magic.",

        <div class="tab-content">
            <a id="top-content-scroll"></a>
            <div class="tab tab-pane active" role="tabpanel" id="mainTabPanel" aria-labelledby="showFullText">
                <article class="article">
                    
                    
                        
                        
                        
                            
                            <div class="hlFld-Fulltext"><div id="abstractId1" class="hlFld-Abstract"><h2 class="section-heading-2" id="abstract">ABSTRACT</h2><p class="last">In 1887, psychical researchers Richard Hodgson and S. J. Davey published a study that revealed counterintuitive aspects of human cognition: How healthy, sober, honest observers of events can be prone to seemingly remarkable errors of eyewitness testimony. Their report, titled ‘The Possibilities of Mal-Observation and Lapse of Memory from a Practical Point of View’ included a collection of eyewitness statements from participants in a series of hoax séances. Hodgson and Davey asserted that all phenomena in their séances were produced using techniques drawn from performance magic. However, their participants were not initially informed about this fact. In their accounts, participants consistently attributed events that they saw, not to trickery, but to spiritual phenomena and psychic powers on the part of Davey, who played the role of the ‘medium’. A major advantage this novel paradigm was that the experimenters had a highly reliable record of actual events, because they had orchestrated the séances themselves. Their paper emphasised the contrasts between what their participants reported remembering relative to what had actually occurred. The errors of the witnesses were marked by failures to notice or remember key aspects of the séance, and even instances where the participants seemed to confabulate- confidently reporting events that had never really taken place. Neither Hodgson nor Davey would have identified themselves as psychologists or cognitive scientists, rather they considered themselves to be psychical researchers, operating under the auspices of the Society for Psychical Research (SPR). At the time, experimental psychology was only just beginning to emerge as a formal and distinct academic discipline. But looking back on their work from a modern perspective, it arguably anticipated more formal theoretical and empirical developments in cognitive psychology by decades. Ironically, given that their report systematically documented failures of attention and lapses of memory, it has been largely ignored in historical considerations of psychology research and is relatively unknown today. In this paper, I will explore the historical context of Hodgson and Davey’s report and discuss how some of their key findings can be related to our contemporary scientific understanding of attention, memory, metacognition, and the science of magic.</p></div><div class="abstractKeywords"><div class="hlFld-KeywordText"><div><p id="kwd-title-d2983901e99" class="kwd-title">KEYWORDS: </p><ul aria-labelledby="kwd-title-d2983901e99"><li><a href="/keyword/Attention" class="kwd-btn keyword-click" kameleoonlistener-jeqi="true">Attention</a></li><li><a href="/keyword/memory" class="kwd-btn keyword-click" kameleoonlistener-quel="true">memory</a></li><li><a href="/keyword/eyewitness+testimony" class="kwd-btn keyword-click" kameleoonlistener-5qyl="true">eyewitness testimony</a></li><li><a href="/keyword/psychical+research" class="kwd-btn keyword-click" kameleoonlistener-v2pp="true">psychical research</a></li><li><a href="/keyword/science+of+magic" class="kwd-btn keyword-click" kameleoonlistener-xtgx="true">science of magic</a></li><li><a href="/keyword/illusion" class="kwd-btn keyword-click" kameleoonlistener-cyud="true">illusion</a></li></ul></div></div></div><div id="embedded-pdf-target" class="with-border"></div><div class="pb-dropzone" data-pb-dropzone="contentNavigationDropZoneAbs"><div class="widget gql-content-navigation none  widget-none" id="2ffb43c7-15dc-4c79-9e29-8e4bed9b3d1e">
        
        

        <div class="wrapped ">
            
            <div class="widget-body body body-none "><div class="ajaxWidget" data-ajax-widget="gql-content-navigation" data-ajax-widget-id="2ffb43c7-15dc-4c79-9e29-8e4bed9b3d1e" data-ajax-observe="true">
    
</div></div>
        </div>
    </div></div><div class="NLM_sec NLM_sec_level_1" id="s0001"><h2 class="section-heading-2" id="d1e120">Introduction</h2><p>In 1887, psychical researchers Richard Hodgson and S. J. Davey published a report detailing a series of experiments designed to examine the reliability of eyewitness testimony.<span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref en"><a data-rid="en0001" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="fn" data-label="footnote"><span class="off-screen">Footnote</span><sup>1</sup></a></span> Their article, ‘The Possibilities of Mal-observation and Lapse of Memory from a Practical Point of View’, (referred to throughout this paper as the Mal-Observation Report) describes how they conducted a series of performances using sleight-of-hand methods to apparently demonstrate supernatural communication with the spirits of the dead. Before explaining that the effects were tricks, they asked their participants to produce written accounts of what they remembered. These written accounts revealed that the participants reliably misperceived and misremembered key aspects of the performances, with many of them being unable to explain the real methods behind the trick and resorting to supernatural explanations. Not only is this one of the first recorded instances of studying participants’ reactions to sleight-of-hand magic tricks, but it is arguably one of the earliest experimental psychological accounts of phenomena now known as reconstructive memory and inattentional blindness, which Hodgson and Davey referred to at the time as <i>mal-observation</i>. Both reconstructive memory and inattentional blindness are concepts that did not receive formal labels or widespread scientific recognition until many years after Hodgson and Davey’s report. Today, the term ‘reconstructive memory’ refers to the concept that our ability to recall past events involves an active reconstruction based on our general knowledge, assumptions, and expectations, as opposed to simply being a faithful reproduction of past experiences.<span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref en"><a data-rid="en0002" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="fn" data-label="footnote"><span class="off-screen">Footnote</span><sup>2</sup></a></span> A reconstructive memory process <i>can</i> result in accurate memories, but a direct consequence of this reconstructive process is that it is prone to errors and false recollections. Traditionally, psychologists attribute the initial theoretical conceptualisation of reconstructive memory to the work of Fredrick Bartlett, a psychologist who is credited with pioneering formal investigations on human memory outside of laboratory settings.<span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref en"><a data-rid="en0003" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="fn" data-label="footnote"><span class="off-screen">Footnote</span><sup>3</sup></a></span> Subsequently, another key body of experimental and theoretical literature on reconstructive memory is attributed to the works of Elizabeth Loftus, which, since the 1970s, empirically demonstrated that reconstructive processes can yield significant and long-lasting changes to reported memories, including the generation of ‘false memories’ – elaborate recollections for events that never actually occurred but are phenomenologically indistinguishable from ‘true’ memories of events that genuinely occurred.<span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref en"><a data-rid="en0004" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="fn" data-label="footnote"><span class="off-screen">Footnote</span><sup>4</sup></a></span> Another phenomenon that would not be formally recognised until decades later, but is nonetheless evident in Hodgson and Davey’s report, is ‘inattentional blindness’. This term describes the fact that we can fail to detect visual objects or events that are fully visible and in plain sight when our attention is otherwise engaged. The term first emerged in the 1990s, when psychologists Mack and Rock reported a series of experiments, wherein they used simple computerised visual displays to demonstrate that participants would often fail to notice obvious visual stimuli.<span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref en"><a data-rid="en0005" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="fn" data-label="footnote"><span class="off-screen">Footnote</span><sup>5</sup></a></span> Later studies have demonstrated that inattentional blindness is not limited to failures to notice simple shapes on a computer monitor, but can also occur in more naturalistic, complex, and dynamic scenes.<span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref en"><a data-rid="en0006" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="fn" data-label="footnote"><span class="off-screen">Footnote</span><sup>6</sup></a></span> Both reconstructive memory and inattentional blindness are deeply counterintuitive psychological concepts that <i>seem</i> to contradict everyday human experience.<span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref en"><a data-rid="en0007" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="fn" data-label="footnote"><span class="off-screen">Footnote</span><sup>7</sup></a></span> It <i>feels</i> impossible that we can generate real-seeming memories of events that never happened, or that we can fail to see seemingly obvious visual events that occur right before our eyes. Such disconnections between how people <i>feel</i> cognition works, and how it actually functions can be classified as metacognitive illusions. Thanks to a large body of careful empirical work that accumulated throughout the twentieth century, inattentional blindness and reconstructive memory are now firmly established to be a natural part of healthy everyday human cognitive processes. But despite the fact that the Hodgson and Davey’s 1887 report systematically described many of these phenomena, it is generally overlooked, aside from rather specialised publications related to magic illusions, anomalistic psychology, or parapsychology.<span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref en"><a data-rid="en0008" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="fn" data-label="footnote"><span class="off-screen">Footnote</span><sup>8</sup></a></span> According to Google Scholar (accessed 14 August 2025), Hodgson and Davey’s report has been cited a total of only 70 times. In contrast, Fredric Bartlett’s <i>Remembering</i> has been cited 28,682 times, Elizabeth Loftus’ most cited review chapter related to false memory formation has been cited 5218 times, and Daniel Simons and Christopher Chabris’ iconic study of inattentional blindness has been cited 4691 times. <span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref en"><a data-rid="en0009" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="fn" data-label="footnote"><span class="off-screen">Footnote</span><sup>9</sup></a></span> In this paper, I aim to make the case that Hodgson and Davey’s Mal-Observation Report is well worth remembering and attending.</p><div class="NLM_sec NLM_sec_level_2" id="s0001-s2001"><h3 class="section-heading-3" id="d1e171">Setting the Stage</h3><p>The Mal-Observation Report was published at a confluence of three historically parallel trends: The rise of ‘Modern Spiritualism’, the popularity of performance magic as entertainment, and the emergence of experimental psychology as a formal scientific and academic discipline.<span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref en"><a data-rid="en0010" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="fn" data-label="footnote"><span class="off-screen">Footnote</span><sup>10</sup></a></span> Modern Spiritualism refers to a socio-cultural religious movement that spread throughout the US and around the world, starting in the mid-1800s. Many religious practices promote the idea that a person’s spirit or soul survives beyond death, but spiritualism set itself apart from more conventional religions in that proponents asserted that the spirits of the dead could physically interact and directly communicate with the living. Furthermore, the miracles of Modern Spiritualism were not relegated to stories of ancient texts, rather they were presented as contemporary, participatory, and, above all, empirical. Anyone could personally witness proof of spiritualistic phenomena; no faith was required.</p><p>As spiritualistic practices spread and flourished in popularity, spiritualistic phenomena allegedly manifested in variety of ways. Early mediums, like the Fox Sisters initially claimed communicated with spirits through ‘raps’ or knocking noises.<span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref en"><a data-rid="en0011" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="fn" data-label="footnote"><span class="off-screen">Footnote</span><sup>11</sup></a></span> But alternative and more spectacular methods of demonstrating spiritual phenomena rapidly proliferated. In the most extreme instances of physical phenomena, spirits were said to visibly manifest in plain view as disembodied faces, hands, or full bodies. Or the spirits might invisibly hoist objects and musical instruments. Spiritualist mediums would claim to ‘channel’ spirits that could provide them with extraordinary abilities, such as access to knowledge that they had no earthly way of knowing or allow them to perform physically impossible feats like levitation.<span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref en"><a data-rid="en0012" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="fn" data-label="footnote"><span class="off-screen">Footnote</span><sup>12</sup></a></span> New modalities of spirit communication likewise emerged. Such methods included table tilting, wherein invisible spirits would appear to shake small tables. In other instances, the spirits might engage in direct voice communication wherein they would speak through the voices of the medium. Sometimes the spirits would cause levitations of objects or even the mediums themselves. Of particular relevance to the present discussion was the practice of ‘psychography’ or independent slate-writing.<span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref en"><a data-rid="en0013" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="fn" data-label="footnote"><span class="off-screen">Footnote</span><sup>13</sup></a></span> In slate-writing séances, the medium would present participants with one or more school slates — small handheld blackboards that were of common use by schoolchildren at that time (<button class="ref show-table-fig-ref" data-id="f0001" data-behaviour="show-popup" data-popup-event-type="fig">Figure 1</button>). The slates would be shown to be free of any marks. And then, under circumstances where it would seem that no one could have physically written on the slates, writing would appear. Such writings were attributed to spirit agency.
</p><div class="figureView"><div class="short-legend"><p class="captionText"><span aria-level="3" role="heading"><span class="captionLabel">Figure 1. </span></span> Example of an ‘ungimmicked’ school slate. This slate was used by upstate New York school children in the late 19<sup>th</sup> or early 20<sup>th</sup> Century.<span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref en"><a data-rid="en0014" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="fn" data-label="footnote"><span class="off-screen">Footnote</span><sup>14</sup></a></span>.</p></div><a href="#" class="thumbnail" data-behaviour="show-popup" data-popup-event-type="fig" data-id="f0001"><img id="d1e205" src="/cms/asset/42a80338-95d0-4946-bd4d-b458bfd37885/ranr_a_2579758_f0001_oc.jpg" loading="lazy" height="500" width="374" alt="Figure 1. Example of an ‘ungimmicked’ school slate. This slate was used by upstate New York school children in the late 19th or early 20th Century.Footnote14."></a><div><button class="show-full-size btn btn-sm" data-behaviour="show-popup" data-id="f0001" data-popup-event-type="fig">Display full size</button></div></div><p></p><p>Several slate-writing mediums achieved celebrity status, making them of particular interest to both the general public and the scientific community. One of the most notorious slate-writing mediums was Dr. Henry Slade. Slade was an American spirit medium who began his career as a ‘clairvoyant physician’ (his title of doctor was not attributable to any academic or medical institution), but he would go on to become internationally famous for his propensity for producing slate-written communications from spirits.<span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref en"><a data-rid="en0015" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="fn" data-label="footnote"><span class="off-screen">Footnote</span><sup>15</sup></a></span> This skillset led him to travel extensively around the world. While visiting London in 1877, he was arrested and tried for fraud.<span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref en"><a data-rid="en0016" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="fn" data-label="footnote"><span class="off-screen">Footnote</span><sup>16</sup></a></span> The trial drew international attention and ended with Slade being convicted of fraud, but then having his conviction overturned on a legal technicality. He fled Britain before he could be re-tried, eventually ending up in Leipzig Germany, where he demonstrated his powers to luminaries from the field psychophysics (a precursor of experimental psychology), including Gustav Fechner, Johann Zöllner, the university’s chair of astrophysics, and Wilhelm Wundt, who would later be dubbed the founding father of experimental psychology.<span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref en"><a data-rid="en0017" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="fn" data-label="footnote"><span class="off-screen">Footnote</span><sup>17</sup></a></span> Over the course of multiple sittings over several weeks, Slade not only demonstrated slate-writing but a variety of phenomena that were (seemingly) unexplainable by conventional physical laws, including the deflection of a compass needle with his mind, tying impossible knots in string, materialising and dematerialising small objects, clairvoyance, and even the manifestation of a tangible spirit hand. Zöllner was impressed with Slade’s abilities, so much so that he proposed a new scientific discipline of <i>Transcendentale Physik</i> [Transcendental Physics] to explain Slade’s mediumship in terms of fourth dimensional geometry.<span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref en"><a data-rid="en0018" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="fn" data-label="footnote"><span class="off-screen">Footnote</span><sup>18</sup></a></span> Zöllner rushed to publish his findings and theories as a multi-volume set of <i>Wissenschaftliche Abhandlungen</i> (‘Scientific Papers’ published from 1877–1878), which contained more than 2000 pages describing his investigations of Slade. The writings stirred controversy not only in Germany but around the world.<span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref en"><a data-rid="en0019" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="fn" data-label="footnote"><span class="off-screen">Footnote</span><sup>19</sup></a></span> In 1880, Charles Carleton Massesy, a British barrister, compiled and translated selections of Zöllner’s German writings and published them under the title <i>Transcendental Physics</i>—the book proved to be popular, particularly with English-speaking spiritualists, and was republished in multiple editions. <span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref en"><a data-rid="en0020" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="fn" data-label="footnote"><span class="off-screen">Footnote</span><sup>20</sup></a></span> Crucially, Zöllner was aware of accusations that Slade was dishonest, but he asserted that Slade was an honest man who was being unfairly slandered by small-minded people.<span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref en"><a data-rid="en0021" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="fn" data-label="footnote"><span class="off-screen">Footnote</span><sup>21</sup></a></span> One such person was Wilhelm Wundt, who had been invited to witness one of Slade’s demonstrations, published an open letter asserting that Slade’s demonstrations could be more rationally dismissed as ‘jugglery’ or sleight-of-hand magic methods.<span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref en"><a data-rid="en0022" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="fn" data-label="footnote"><span class="off-screen">Footnote</span><sup>22</sup></a></span> However, despite rhetorically gesturing towards the idea of magic trick methods, Wundt, as a non-magician himself, did not offer any substantive methodological explanations for how Slade might have accomplished such convincing tricks. Zöllner continued to promote Transcendental Physics until his death in 1882, while Wundt went on to establish the world’s first-ever formally recognised laboratory of experimental psychology at Leipzig in 1883.<span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref en"><a data-rid="en0023" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="fn" data-label="footnote"><span class="off-screen">Footnote</span><sup>23</sup></a></span></p><p>In developing his lab and legacy, Wundt never returned to investigations of magic and spiritual fraud. Arguably, his and other psychologists’ disengagement from psychical research represented a deliberate strategic effort to distance the nascent field of experimental psychology from associations with spiritual phenomena.<span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref en"><a data-rid="en0024" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="fn" data-label="footnote"><span class="off-screen">Footnote</span><sup>24</sup></a></span> But other researchers were more eager to explore both magic tricks and the scientific claims associated with the modern spiritualism movement. In 1882, a group of scholars in Cambridge banded together to form the Society for Psychical Research (SPR), with the goal of investigating phenomena associated with the spiritualist movement. The membership came to include scientific luminaries like Alfred Russell Wallace, William Crookes, William James, and Oliver Lodge. The SPR was specifically established to engage with spiritualist claims, including mediumship, apparitional experiences, haunted houses, and the survival of the soul after bodily death. The SPR’s main mechanism for disseminating its findings was through printed publications. The society produced a monthly <i>Journal of the Society for Psychical Research</i> that was intended exclusively to members, and they also produced a public facing series of <i>Proceedings of the Society of Psychical Research</i>, which were intended for public consumption. The exact nature of the SPR’s distribution numbers in the 1880s are not explicitly documented in contemporary publications of their <i>Journal</i> or <i>Proceedings</i>. However, in a recorded speech given in 1885 by the SPR’s then president, Balfour Stewart, he remarked that:
</p><div class="quote"><p class="first last">In reply to the question, what has the Society done? I may state that since its commencement it has issued seven parts of <i>Proceedings</i>, of which a total number exceeding 12,000&nbsp;has been distributed to Members and others, placed in public libraries, sent for review, and sold through the ordinary channels.<span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref en"><a data-rid="en0025" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="fn" data-label="footnote"><span class="off-screen">Footnote</span><sup>25</sup></a></span></p></div><p></p><p>Advertisements for the SPR’s <i>Proceedings</i> could be found in regional newspapers. For example, the issue of the <i>Proceedings</i> featuring the Mal-Observation Report could be ordered by anyone for the price of 4 shillings<span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref en"><a data-rid="en0026" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="fn" data-label="footnote"><span class="off-screen">Footnote</span><sup>26</sup></a></span> (<button class="ref show-table-fig-ref" data-id="f0002" data-behaviour="show-popup" data-popup-event-type="fig">Figure 2</button>), about 25 pounds or 29 euros in today’s currency<span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref en"><a data-rid="en0027" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="fn" data-label="footnote"><span class="off-screen">Footnote</span><sup>27</sup></a></span> (Bank of England, <span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="cit0004" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference"><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>2025</a></span>). The SPR members’ methods of communicating their findings and discussions through printed media, both internally and externally, arguably reveal important patterns in the ways that researchers have presented debates about scientific developments. The impacts of the practices were not limited to the domain of paranormal studies: Lamont identifies these scientific debates around spiritualism as a Victorian <i>crisis of evidence</i> that would directly shape the development of modern conventions of academic psychology.<span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref en"><a data-rid="en0028" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="fn" data-label="footnote"><span class="off-screen">Footnote</span><sup>28</sup></a></span> Similarly, Efram Sera-Shriar has identified the SPR’s debates around <i>credible witnessing</i> – the question of who has the authority to make qualified observations of extraordinary phenomena — as re-shaping academic conventions of anthropology. <span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref en"><a data-rid="en0029" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="fn" data-label="footnote"><span class="off-screen">Footnote</span><sup>29</sup></a></span> Furthermore, Noakes has demonstrated how such debates and investigations influenced the modern practice of physics. <span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref en"><a data-rid="en0030" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="fn" data-label="footnote"><span class="off-screen">Footnote</span><sup>30</sup></a></span>
</p><div class="figureView"><div class="short-legend"><p class="captionText"><span aria-level="3" role="heading"><span class="captionLabel">Figure 2. </span></span> Image of a Publishers Column advertising the issue of the SPR’s <i>Proceedings</i> featuring Hodgson and Davey’s Mal-Observation Report<span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref en"><a data-rid="en0031" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="fn" data-label="footnote"><span class="off-screen">Footnote</span><sup>31</sup></a></span>.</p></div><a href="#" class="thumbnail" data-behaviour="show-popup" data-popup-event-type="fig" data-id="f0002"><img id="d1e322" src="/cms/asset/46726c3f-84df-4baf-8f31-b14f9b7e26e1/ranr_a_2579758_f0002_b.gif" loading="lazy" height="500" width="489" alt="Figure 2. Image of a Publishers Column advertising the issue of the SPR’s Proceedings featuring Hodgson and Davey’s Mal-Observation ReportFootnote31."></a><div><button class="show-full-size btn btn-sm" data-behaviour="show-popup" data-id="f0002" data-popup-event-type="fig">Display full size</button></div></div><p></p><p>Partially as a result of Henry Slade’s notoriety, the SPR became particularly concerned with the practice of psychography, and the value of evidence obtained by testimonies of this extraordinary phenomenon. Hodgson and Davey’s Mal-Observation Report was largely inspired by controversies related to eyewitness accounts of slate-writing séances. They were concerned, not only with accounts of Slade’s feats, but also with the alleged phenomena of William Eglinton,<span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref en"><a data-rid="en0032" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="fn" data-label="footnote"><span class="off-screen">Footnote</span><sup>32</sup></a></span> a British medium who developed his own reputation for slate-writing after Slade had fled England following his trial for fraud. The SPR received and published numerous accounts of extraordinary test sittings from various members of the public who had witnessed Eglinton in action.<span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref en"><a data-rid="en0033" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="fn" data-label="footnote"><span class="off-screen">Footnote</span><sup>33</sup></a></span> Eglinton would use slates that had been provided by the witness, and having placed a small piece of chalk between two blank slates, would produce messages. These were words selected by the participants, sometimes seemingly randomly chosen books that Eglinton had apparently no way of seeing. Frank Podmore, one of the SPR’s founding members wrote, in 1902, that ‘In the history of the movement, no physical manifestation had ever won such universal recognition’ as Eglinton’s abilities.<span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref en"><a data-rid="en0034" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="fn" data-label="footnote"><span class="off-screen">Footnote</span><sup>34</sup></a></span> Accounts of Eglinton’s mediumship sparked a fiery debate in the pages of the SPR’s publications. Eleanor Mildred Sidgwick published a sceptical paper cautioning that the Society should never seriously consider accounts of mediums who have ever been credibly accused of fraud, and she specifically accused Eglinton of having been caught faking manifestations. She wrote that he had been caught with props, including muslin drapes and a false beard that were clearly intended to be used for creating hoax apparitions. Sidgwick attributed the remarkable accounts of his activities to a combination of conjuring (i.e., magic trick) methods being deployed by the medium and ‘mal-observation’ on the part of witnesses (i.e., perceptual and memory illusions).<span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref en"><a data-rid="en0035" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="fn" data-label="footnote"><span class="off-screen">Footnote</span><sup>35</sup></a></span> Her sceptical assertions were met with a scepticism on the part of Eglinton’s supporters. Charles Carleton Massey (who had produced the English translations of Zöllner’s investigations of Henry Slade) directly responded to Sidgwick by asserting that mal-observation could not possibly account for the phenomena allegedly produced by Eglinton. ‘Most of us would feel ashamed’, he wrote, ‘of mistaking a conjurer’s trick for a genuine manifestation of unknown force’.<span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref en"><a data-rid="en0036" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="fn" data-label="footnote"><span class="off-screen">Footnote</span><sup>36</sup></a></span> Magicians, he wrote, could only accomplish their deceptions under conditions that were highly inferior to that of a test medium. According to Massey, in séances, participants generally knew exactly what phenomena were going to be produced. Magicians, he argued, would be unable to successfully perform illusion if their audiences were aware of exactly what tricks they would be performing; such uncertainty ‘does not and cannot exist’.<span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref en"><a data-rid="en0037" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="fn" data-label="footnote"><span class="off-screen">Footnote</span><sup>37</sup></a></span> If deception was possible for Eglinton during his slate-writing séances, Massey argued that the only reasonable conclusion would be that all accounts based on human observation would have to be considered ‘absolutely worthless’.<span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref en"><a data-rid="en0038" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="fn" data-label="footnote"><span class="off-screen">Footnote</span><sup>38</sup></a></span> He appealed to his readers to consider that the witnesses to Eglinton (and other mediums) could not be so easily fooled. While he acknowledged that an honest witness might sometimes forget certain facts and events, he argued that they could never honestly ‘<i>invent</i> specific and positive events of perception’.<span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref en"><a data-rid="en0039" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="fn" data-label="footnote"><span class="off-screen">Footnote</span><sup>39</sup></a></span> Massey’s sentiments were representative of other scientists who espoused the validity and importance of spiritualistic phenomena. For example, Alfred Russell Wallace, a scientific luminary in the field of biology, known for co-developing the theory of natural selection along with Charles Darwin, had written that the miracles of spiritualism had ‘been tested and examined by sceptics of every grade of incredulity, men in every way qualified to detect imposture or discover natural causes- trained physicists, medical men, lawyers, and men of business- but in every case the investigators have retired baffled, or become converts’.<span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref en"><a data-rid="en0040" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="fn" data-label="footnote"><span class="off-screen">Footnote</span><sup>40</sup></a></span></p><p class="last">In response to this line of argumentation, Sidgwick turned to Angelo John Lewis, a barrister and a semi-professional magician who performed and wrote under the stage name Professor Hoffmann. Lewis not only examined written accounts by Eglinton’s witnesses, but also personally attended twelve sittings with Eglinton. In describing his analysis of the witness accounts of Eglinton’s phenomena<span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref en"><a data-rid="en0041" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="fn" data-label="footnote"><span class="off-screen">Footnote</span>41</a></span><sup>,</sup><span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref en"><a data-rid="en0042" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="fn" data-label="footnote"><span class="off-screen">Footnote</span><sup>42</sup></a></span>, Lewis was careful to acknowledge that he was open to the possibility of genuine spiritual phenomena, but wrote that in his opinion as a magician, the witness accounts were indeed suggestive of errors of observation that might be attributable to conjuring deceptions. He noted that witnesses to magic performances did not necessarily describe events that they saw, but rather events that they imagined they saw. He also acknowledged that even highly trained magicians could still be fooled by conjuring methods that they were not personally familiar with or not expecting. As to Lewis’ personal experiences sitting with Eglinton, it transpired that throughout repeated attempts ‘the spirits obstinately declined to manifest’. Such blank séances presented the debating researchers with something of a rhetorical stalemate. Proponents of spiritualism could argue that the uneventful sittings were evidence in favour of the genuineness of mediums, after all, if the medium was resorting to conjuring then, surely, they would always be able to perform their tricks. And thus, the unreliability of their mediumship supported the idea that reported spiritual phenomena were logically attributable to forces outside of the mediums’ direct control. On the other hand, sceptics could argue that fraudulent mediums would deliberately avoid using trickery if they suspected they were being observed by a sitter with conjuring expertise (as Lewis was well known for). And the medium could simply wait to perform their deceptions until they were in the presence of a more gullible audience, while claiming that the blank séances were outside their control.</p></div></div><div class="NLM_sec NLM_sec_level_1" id="s0002"><h2 class="section-heading-2" id="d1e365">Hodgson and Davey’s Mal-Observation Report</h2><p>Hodgson and Davey’s Mal-Observation Report describes a scientific investigation that they developed as a way of around the empirical problem of investigating the reliability of testimonies related allegedly genuine spiritualist mediumship. By using magic trick methods to simulate slate-writing phenomena, they avoided the problem of blank séances that had plagued Lewis’ investigation. Furthermore, by relying exclusively on magic trick methods performed by an openly <i>fake</i> medium who made no claim to supernormal abilities, they (vainly) hoped to avoid the potential criticism that any genuine psychical phenomena might occur during their experiments. Hodgson took care to declare that even though the participants testimonies might seem ‘marvellous enough’ to support the hypothesis of occult agency, ‘the “psychographic” phenomena described in the following records are conjuring, and only conjuring, performances’.<span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref en"><a data-rid="en0043" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="fn" data-label="footnote"><span class="off-screen">Footnote</span><sup>43</sup></a></span> ‘The object of the notes’, he wrote, was not to educate readers about particular conjuring methods, but ‘to show to investigators the kind and degree of mistakes which may be made by educated and intelligent witnesses in recording their impression of a performance the main lines of which are planned with the deliberate intention of deceiving them, but few, if any, of the details of which can be described as absolutely fixed’.<span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref en"><a data-rid="en0044" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="fn" data-label="footnote"><span class="off-screen">Footnote</span><sup>44</sup></a></span></p><p>The Mal-Observation Report represents the synthesis of Hodgson’s first-hand experience debunking professional mediums and Davey’s conjuring expertise. Richard Hodgson (<button class="ref show-table-fig-ref" data-id="f0003" data-behaviour="show-popup" data-popup-event-type="fig">Figure 3</button>) was born in Melbourne, Australia and obtained a degree in Law from Cambridge University.<span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref en"><a data-rid="en0046" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="fn" data-label="footnote"><span class="off-screen">Footnote</span><sup>46</sup></a></span> During his time at Cambridge, Hodgson joined the recently formed SPR. As a member of the society, Hodgson had developed reputation as a talented detective debunker of spiritualist mediums. He spearheaded one of the SPR’s first major investigations into spiritualism when he travelled to India to investigate Countess Helena Petrova de Blavatsky, a Russian occultist who had attracted a significant following based on her alleged supernatural abilities. As a founder of the Theosophy movement, Blavatsky claimed that she was directly channelling the spiritual teachings of dead mystics to develop what has variously been described as an occult or new religious system.<span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref en"><a data-rid="en0047" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="fn" data-label="footnote"><span class="off-screen">Footnote</span><sup>47</sup></a></span> Hodgson spent two months in India attempting to verify Blavatsky’s claims. In his eventual report, which he published through the SPR in 1885, he declared that the phenomena that she claimed credit for were entirely fraudulent.<span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref en"><a data-rid="en0048" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="fn" data-label="footnote"><span class="off-screen">Footnote</span><sup>48</sup></a></span> According to Hodgson, the letters Blavatsky claimed to receive from spirits of the dead were written in her own hand, and the spirit forms she manifested were simply human accomplices wearing disguises. Hodgson asserted that Blavatsky’s followers were ‘excessively credulous and deficient in observation’, and that she was actually the centre of a ‘huge fraudulent system’.<span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref en"><a data-rid="en0049" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="fn" data-label="footnote"><span class="off-screen">Footnote</span><sup>49</sup></a></span> Hodgson’s experiences investigating Blavatsky arguably helped spark his initial interest in the relationship between magic tricks and eyewitness testimony, and after meeting S. J. Davey, he would go on to co-develop the mal-observation paradigm to put their ideas to an empirical test.
</p><div class="figureView"><div class="short-legend"><p class="captionText"><span aria-level="3" role="heading"><span class="captionLabel">Figure 3. </span></span> A spirit portrait featuring Richard Hodgson. Taken with the purpose of demonstrating how to create fraudulent spirit photographs.<span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref en"><a data-rid="en0045" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="fn" data-label="footnote"><span class="off-screen">Footnote</span><sup>45</sup></a></span>.</p></div><a href="#" class="thumbnail" data-behaviour="show-popup" data-popup-event-type="fig" data-id="f0003"><img id="d1e404" src="/cms/asset/ea50716c-9e68-4fc3-bbed-f7cd9478f0e5/ranr_a_2579758_f0003_oc.jpg" loading="lazy" height="500" width="354" alt="Figure 3. A spirit portrait featuring Richard Hodgson. Taken with the purpose of demonstrating how to create fraudulent spirit photographs.Footnote45."></a><div><button class="show-full-size btn btn-sm" data-behaviour="show-popup" data-id="f0003" data-popup-event-type="fig">Display full size</button></div></div><p></p><p>Hodgson’s co-experimenter, Samuel John Davey (<button class="ref show-table-fig-ref" data-id="f0004" data-behaviour="show-popup" data-popup-event-type="fig">Figure 4</button>), was an amateur magician. Before he began practising magic, Davey had been a true believer in the reality of spiritualist phenomena. He wrote that he initially came to his beliefs after seeing a recently deceased friend in a dream, and that his beliefs were further inspired by reading an English translation of Zöllner’s <i>Transcendental Physics</i>.<span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref en"><a data-rid="en0050" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="fn" data-label="footnote"><span class="off-screen">Footnote</span><sup>50</sup></a></span> He also personally witnessed the apparently miraculous talents of the medium William Eglinton. Davey was particularly fascinated by the phenomenon of slate-writing. In the Mal-Observation Report, Davey confessed that, for a time, he actually came to believe that he himself possessed genuine supernatural talents. He experimented in trying to receive messages from spirits by leaving blank slates in locked drawers in his house. He was astonished upon examining some of the slates to find that messages had appeared on the slates that he himself had cleaned and locked away. Eventually, he came to realise that his friends were hoaxing him by secretly marking the slates themselves through entirely natural means. This experience of being tricked led Davey to begin studying conjuring. And, consequently, he came to appreciate that many of the effects that he had previously considered to be miraculous were actually attributable to magic trick methods. Davey soon progressed beyond academic curiosity about magic and began putting his conjuring knowledge into practice. He soon came to appreciate that the errors of observation that witnesses made of his own performances might very well parallel the kinds of errors he now thought that he had once made when observing mediums:
</p><div class="quote"><p class="first last">I noticed that many persons made statements concerning my performances, as to the conditions of the production of the writing, which were just as emphatic as I made in my own reports about Eglinton, and I also noticed that nearly all these statements were entirely wrong. Even when I sometimes revealed the fact that I was merely a conjurer, the reply which I frequently received was something of this kind: ‘Yes, you may say it is conjuring, but it could not have been done by that means when I did so-and-so’ (describing a supposed test) ‘and yet we got the writing all the same’.<span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref en"><a data-rid="en0051" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="fn" data-label="footnote"><span class="off-screen">Footnote</span><sup>51</sup></a></span></p></div>
<div class="figureView"><div class="short-legend"><p class="captionText"><span aria-level="3" role="heading"><span class="captionLabel">Figure 4. </span></span> A woodcut portrait of S. J. Davey (Scott &amp; Davey, 1891, frontispiece). This image, the only known image of S. J. Davey, was recently re-discovered by Richard Wiseman (see Wiseman <span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref bibr"><a data-rid="cit0118" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="bibr" data-label="reference"><span class="off-screen">Citation</span>2025</a></span>).<span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref en"><a data-rid="en0052" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="fn" data-label="footnote"><span class="off-screen">Footnote</span><sup>52</sup></a></span>.</p></div><a href="#" class="thumbnail" data-behaviour="show-popup" data-popup-event-type="fig" data-id="f0004"><img id="d1e436" src="/cms/asset/c1ea5045-e2a6-41e2-a509-50a485140a6a/ranr_a_2579758_f0004_oc.jpg" loading="lazy" height="500" width="446" alt="Figure 4. A woodcut portrait of S. J. Davey (Scott &amp; Davey, 1891, frontispiece). This image, the only known image of S. J. Davey, was recently re-discovered by Richard Wiseman (see Wiseman Citation2025).Footnote52."></a><div><button class="show-full-size btn btn-sm" data-behaviour="show-popup" data-id="f0004" data-popup-event-type="fig">Display full size</button></div></div><p></p><p>Davey approached the Society for Psychical Research to demonstrate his developing conjuring skills. And after witnessing Davey’s tricks, Richard Hodgson and Eleanor Sidgwick realised that Davey’s pseudo-mediumship represented an opportunity for them to develop a new method to empirically investigate testimonies of alleged psychic phenomena.</p><p>The Mal-Observation Report opens with a general introduction written by Hodgson describing his own experiences investigating psychic fraud. He professed having once been very confident that eyewitness testimonies of séances represented examples of genuine phenomena. However, over time, as he learned more about the possibilities of trickery and conjuring methods, he grew more and more sceptical. Hodgson directly addresses both Alfred Russell Wallace’s and Charles Carleton Massey’s assertions that testimonies of personal experiences could be used to definitively establish the empirical reality of spiritualism. <span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref en"><a data-rid="en0053" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="fn" data-label="footnote"><span class="off-screen">Footnote</span><sup>53</sup></a></span> He described how his personal experiences of seeing witnesses being fooled by illusions, combined with his own empirical investigations with Davey, suggested that human testimony, particularly in the context magic performances, was much less reliable than past researchers had appreciated. The second part of the Mal-Observation Report, the Experimental Investigation, was authored by Davey. He began by briefly describing his own personal journey from credulity to scepticism before presenting a series of written statements from the participants, accompanied by his own meta-commentary. Over the course of several months, Hodgson and Davey had invited various members of the public to attend séances hosted by Davey. Most, but not all the séances took place in Davey’s home. The séances were loosely scripted and would vary between different audiences as Davey improvised around individual circumstances and reactions. However, at the start of each performance, he would always specifically caution his audience to be mindful of any form of trickery or deception. However, he would not specifically tell them that he was going to be presenting magic tricks, nor did he ever explicitly attribute his performance to supernatural powers. At the conclusion of each demonstration, audience members were asked to provide written accounts in the form of letters, describing everything that they had experienced. Hodgson explained the motivation for their experiments by making direct reference to the debate between Sidgwick and Massey:
</p><div class="quote"><p class="first last">It appeared that a large number of the readers of Mrs. Sidgwick’s article in the Journal for June were prejudiced in favour of ordinary’ human powers of observation and recollection under - it is to be remembered -exceptionally adverse circumstances; and that they were thus prejudiced simply because they had never made any special experiments, with the view of ascertaining exactly how much reliance could be placed upon the reports of even acute and intelligent observers of the ‘slate-writing’ performances of a conjurer known as such. They had decided a priori as to the capacity of human perception and memory under quite peculiar conditions, and most of them, I venture to say, had thus decided, not only without possessing any familiarity with the various modes of producing’ slate-writing’ by conjuring, but without possessing any familiarity with conjuring tricks in general, and without being aware of the extent to which we are all subject to <i>illusions of Memory</i>, which, in relation to the reports of ‘psychography’, are more deserving of consideration than even illusions of Perception.<span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref en"><a data-rid="en0054" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="fn" data-label="footnote"><span class="off-screen">Footnote</span><sup>54</sup></a></span></p></div><p></p><p>In total, the Mal-Observation Report collected 27 distinct accounts from 17 separate performances (<button class="ref show-table-fig-ref" data-id="f0005" data-behaviour="show-popup" data-popup-event-type="fig">Figure 5</button>). Each witness account was reproduced in full and was accompanied by commentary from Hodgson and Davey about the ways that the participants’ reports deviated from the actual events. Participants generally endorsed Davey as a genuine medium, and they made references to the inexplicability of his performances. For example, part of an account from one Mrs. M. Johnson, written in September 1886, reads as follows:
</p><div class="quote"><p class="first last">My sisters and I being most interested in the subject of slate-writing and anxious to see something of it, Mr. Davey kindly arranged a meeting at his house. We sat at an ordinary table in a well-lighted room, and writing was quickly produced on the inner surface of one of two slates held firmly together, once by Mr. Davey and myself, at other times by my sister and Mr. Davey; at first just under the edge of the table, then above, and afterwards on one of my sister’s shoulders. This was the more wonderful as we had purchased the slates on our way from the station. Of course between the slates were placed three points of different coloured chalks, after which Mr. Davey asked us in which colour the writing should appear, and it did so in the colour we elected, the slate being covered with writing. We are all quite certain that the slates were never out of the hands of one or other of us, and we are totally unable to account for the slate-writing.<span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref en"><a data-rid="en0055" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="fn" data-label="footnote"><span class="off-screen">Footnote</span><sup>55</sup></a></span></p></div>
<div class="figureView"><div class="short-legend"><p class="captionText"><span aria-level="3" role="heading"><span class="captionLabel">Figure 5. </span></span> An illustrated reproduction of Mr. Davey’s latched slates from pages 466–467 of Hodgson and Davey’s Mal-Observation Report.</p></div><a href="#" class="thumbnail" data-behaviour="show-popup" data-popup-event-type="fig" data-id="f0005"><img id="d1e470" src="/cms/asset/db763c39-2767-4176-b573-26b9e52e9f60/ranr_a_2579758_f0005_oc.jpg" loading="lazy" height="388" width="500" alt="Figure 5. An illustrated reproduction of Mr. Davey’s latched slates from pages 466–467 of Hodgson and Davey’s Mal-Observation Report."></a><div><button class="show-full-size btn btn-sm" data-behaviour="show-popup" data-id="f0005" data-popup-event-type="fig">Display full size</button></div></div><p></p><p>Another account by Mr. John H. Rait, concerning a sitting that took place on the 8 September 1896, described in detail how Mr. Davey had inexplicably produced writing between slates that had been securely locked together. Mr. Rait concluded that: ‘Trickery to my mind is utterly impossible in any respect. How it is all done I cannot tell; my advice to the “sceptics” is “go and judge for yourselves”’.<span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref en"><a data-rid="en0056" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="fn" data-label="footnote"><span class="off-screen">Footnote</span><sup>56</sup></a></span> Likewise, an account written by a ‘Mrs. Y’ of sitting that occurred on 10 September 1896 reports how Davey produced writing on a clean slate while she carefully watched his hands for any sign of movement, and how he also produced writing between two locked slates that matched a line from a random page in a book that she had freely selected from a shelf in the room. And how he had caused small fragments of chalk, isolated under a clear tumbler to apparently animate in front of her eyes. She finished her account by stating: ‘I consider the test conditions to have been perfect throughout, and see no possible explanation for the very remarkable phenomena that occurred’.<span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref en"><a data-rid="en0057" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="fn" data-label="footnote"><span class="off-screen">Footnote</span><sup>57</sup></a></span></p><p>Overall, the participants committed consistent and systematic errors in their testimonies: The reports typically omitted crucial elements of the séances — specifically elements that were instrumental for the accomplishment of the conjuring tricks. For example, in one of the scripted segments of the séance Davey would surreptitiously switch a blank slate for another slate that had been pre-marked with writing before the experiment began. Davey conducted most of his performances while seated at a table. He would begin by showing the blank slate clearly; he would then place the slate under the table, where he would switch it for the slate that had writing on it that had previously been concealed beneath the table. He would then remove the slate with writing on it under the table and leave the genuinely blank slate behind. To help conceal the switch, he would not reveal the writing immediately. Instead, he would pretend that the slate was still blank, and he would explicitly express his disappointment that no spirits had been in contact. This ruse was accepted by his audiences, who remembered examining the original slate. Next, Davey would place the pre-written slate on top the table (writing side down) and ask the sitter(s) to place their hands upon it. Davey would then wait for a brief time and then ask them to turn over the slate. At this point, the writing was revealed to the sitters. He would then pretend that the writing had just manifested <i>while</i> the participants’ hands were on the slate. The trick was effective because the participants failed to recognise the significance of the moment when Davey placed the slates under the table, and they consistently failed to record this moment in their accounts of the séance. Thus, participants’ written accounts accurately reflected their experience of seeing writing appear on the slate under seemingly impossible conditions, but they also failed to accurately reflect the reality of what had actually happened. As a result of these omissions, even a reader well versed in fraudulent slate-writing methods would struggle to provide an explanation of how the writing had appeared.</p><p>The Mal-Observation Report’s documentation of witnesses’ omissions is very similar to the idea of inattentional blindness<span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref en"><a data-rid="en0058" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="fn" data-label="footnote"><span class="off-screen">Footnote</span><sup>58</sup></a></span> - a psychological phenomenon that was not formally identified until over a hundred years after the Mal-Observation Report. Davey’s actions of placing the slate beneath the table were clearly visible and in plain sight of the participants. Yet their belief that the writing was produced while they held the slates and their omission of the action of placing the slate under the table indicates that participants nonetheless failed to detect these significant actions. The fact that participants were instructed to watch his actions closely, and clearly believed they had done so, suggests that, at least in some instances, their failures to detect the secret methods may have been related to having their attention otherwise engaged. However, given the fact that the reports were collected some time after the critical events, it is not possible to definitively distinguish between failures of awareness that might have occurred <i>during</i> the performance and subsequent failures of memory that might have occurred <i>after</i> the performance.</p><p>Beyond documenting omissions in the participants’ testimonies, the report also demonstrated that participants engaged in ‘positive’ errors; they confabulated some events that were the products of their own imaginations. For example, one of the participants, Mr. Padshah, described his experience in Sitting IV of the experiment.<span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref en"><a data-rid="en0059" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="fn" data-label="footnote"><span class="off-screen">Footnote</span><sup>59</sup></a></span> According to Padshah, the most remarkable part of his experience was when the written image of his childhood nickname appeared unexpectedly on a slate: ‘ … On the large slate, instead of the numbers we wanted, we got written “Boorzu”. Now this as it happens is the original Persian, the modern corruption of which is my initial name’.<span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref en"><a data-rid="en0060" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="fn" data-label="footnote"><span class="off-screen">Footnote</span><sup>60</sup></a></span>This was particularly impressive to Padshah, because he had not been called this name in many years, and, to his knowledge, no one else present in the room was aware that he had ever been called that. In a note, Hodgson explained exactly how this had <i>actually</i> happened was that Davey had secretly written the word ‘BOOKS’ on the slate to suggest a further test (i.e., a prepared trick) involving books in the room. But when Padshah saw the scrawled word, he mis-read it as ‘BOORZU’, and this minor proofreading error led him to the extraordinary conclusion that a spiritual entity was addressing him personally by a name that no one else in the room, including the medium, could possibly have known by natural means. Davey quickly improvised by wiping the word away before Padshah or anyone else could get a clear look at it, and the other participants were highly impressed by Padshah’s dramatic reaction. One Mr. Russell, who had also been attending the same sitting as a witness, noted in his report:
</p><div class="quote"><p class="first last">Mr. D. now said that a start having been made, more success might be looked for, so the experiment was repeated, the slate being brought up at interval of from 5 to 10 minutes. The first time it had the letters BOORZ written upon it, which Mr. P. explained were the first five letters of his Christian name which was in Persian written BOORZU. Neither Mr. Hodgson, Mr. Hughes, my wife, nor myself had ever heard of this name before.<span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref en"><a data-rid="en0061" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="fn" data-label="footnote"><span class="off-screen">Footnote</span><sup>61</sup></a></span></p></div><p></p><p>In general, contemporary researchers and commentators including the Mal-Observation Reports authors, seemed open to the idea that errors of omission, i.e., forgetfulness and inattention, were fairly commonplace; however, the idea that honest, healthy, sober, intelligent witnesses could form reports based on memories of falsely remembered events was highly controversial. Yet the fact that people can and do create false memories of imagined events that can feel subjectively indistinguishable from ‘real’ memories of actual events is exactly what the evidence from Hodgson and Davey’s study indicated, and this idea has been borne out by subsequent experimental psychological research that took place many years afterwards.<span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref en"><a data-rid="en0062" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="fn" data-label="footnote"><span class="off-screen">Footnote</span><sup>62</sup></a></span></p><div class="NLM_sec NLM_sec_level_2" id="s0002-s2001"><h3 class="section-heading-3" id="d1e517">Initial Responses to the Report and the Follow-Up Article Regarding Davey’s Methods</h3><p>Reactions to the Mal-Observation Report, following its publication, also indicate robust failures of metacognition in some of its readers. A lively written debate was recorded in the SPR’s Journal throughout the following years. One of the writers who actively engaged in the discussion around the Mal-Observation Report was Alfred Russell Wallace, who was, at the time, something of a scientific celebrity, having recently co-discovered the theory of evolution with Charles Darwin.<span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref en"><a data-rid="en0063" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="fn" data-label="footnote"><span class="off-screen">Footnote</span><sup>63</sup></a></span> While Wallace did not personally attend any of Davey’s séances, he attended similar slate-writing demonstrations by other mediums including Henry Slate and William Eglinton. Wallace was certain that he had personally witnessed genuine supernatural phenomena in the presence of various mediums. Even after Hodgson and Davey had explicitly written that they had been using magic trick methods, Wallace remained convinced that the séances conducted by Davey could potentially have involved genuine spiritual phenomena. He wrote an angry letter to the SPR suggesting that Hodgson and Davey were engaged in a deliberate cover-up of Davey’s mediumistic talents. Wallace took particular issue with the fact that the original report did not provide full methodological explanations of the conjuring techniques that were used to accomplish the simulated psychography phenomena. He went so far as to state that ‘unless all [the reported phenomena] can be so explained many of us will be confirmed in our belief that Mr. Davey was really a medium as well as a conjurer, and that in imputing all his performances to “trick” he was deceiving the Society and the public’.<span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref en"><a data-rid="en0064" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="fn" data-label="footnote"><span class="off-screen">Footnote</span><sup>64</sup></a></span> Wallace’s critique neatly illustrates the unfortunate potential consequences of actively employing deception as a tool to demonstrate how powerful deceptive methods can be. Similar issues have occurred throughout history, when magicians have attempted to use performance magic to promote scepticism of extraordinary claims. Arthur Conan Doyle, a committed spiritualist, famously maintained that his friend, the magician Harry Houdini, had accomplished his iconic escapes using psychic powers; this was all in spite of Houdini’s explicit disavowal and active debunking of psychic phenomena.<span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref en"><a data-rid="en0065" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="fn" data-label="footnote"><span class="off-screen">Footnote</span><sup>65</sup></a></span> Similar issues have continued to haunt those who use magic methods and deceptions as tools to explore belief in paranormal phenomena. In the 1980s, the magician James Randi orchestrated an elaborate hoax to publicly discredit the scientific rigour of contemporary parapsychological researchers. Randi’s methods involved having magicians infiltrate a lab posing as test subjects.<span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref en"><a data-rid="en0066" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="fn" data-label="footnote"><span class="off-screen">Footnote</span><sup>66</sup></a></span> In a carefully written critique of Randi’s efforts, sociologist (and magician) Marcello Truzzi actually drew explicit parallels to Hodgson and Davey’s report to help illuminate the issues around using fraudulent methods in efforts to expose fraud, noting that, logically, ‘ An irony in such cases is that once the defrauders admit to their fraud, the door is open to distrust them entirely’.<span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref en"><a data-rid="en0067" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="fn" data-label="footnote"><span class="off-screen">Footnote</span><sup>67</sup></a></span> More recently, in 2019, David Baker reported in <i>Wired</i> magazine about his experiences observing a psychology experiment where research students were instructed to assess the abilities of an allegedly psychic medium.<span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref en"><a data-rid="en0068" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="fn" data-label="footnote"><span class="off-screen">Footnote</span><sup>68</sup></a></span> The experiment was designed to explore how the participants formed beliefs in response to falsely presented empirical evidence.<span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref en"><a data-rid="en0069" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="fn" data-label="footnote"><span class="off-screen">Footnote</span><sup>69</sup></a></span> At the conclusion of the experiment session, the researchers conducted a ‘dehoaxing’ procedure wherein they explicitly revealed that the ‘psychic’ was actually a semi-professional magician (also the author of this paper) who had been using store-bought magic tricks to create <i>illusions</i> of paranormal phenomena. However, when Baker interviewed participants <i>after</i> this dehoaxing, some of them still maintained that what they saw could have been a demonstration of genuine psychic abilities: ‘I still think there was something weird about that guy. He could read minds’.<span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref en"><a data-rid="en0070" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="fn" data-label="footnote"><span class="off-screen">Footnote</span><sup>70</sup></a></span></p><p>Davey personally resisted fully revealing details of his trick methods.<span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref en"><a data-rid="en0071" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="fn" data-label="footnote"><span class="off-screen">Footnote</span><sup>71</sup></a></span> According to Hodgson, Davey worried that revealing specific methods might potentially lead other investigators to become overly confident when attempting to debunk professional mediums. And he was further concerned that, if people interested in committing future frauds read about his methods, they would then be able to adapt their own performances to be even more deceptive. He also wished to continue conducting more eyewitness experiments using the same methods that had been used in the Mal-Observation Report. Sadly, Davey did not have the opportunity to publish further research. He died of typhoid in 1891 at the age of 27.<span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref en"><a data-rid="en0072" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="fn" data-label="footnote"><span class="off-screen">Footnote</span><sup>72</sup></a></span> Shortly after Davey’s death, Hodgson acceded to Wallace and other commentators’ demands and published a follow-up article, titled <i>Mr. Davey’s Imitations by Conjuring of Phenomena Sometimes Attributed to Spirit Agency</i>.<span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref en"><a data-rid="en0073" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="fn" data-label="footnote"><span class="off-screen">Footnote</span><sup>73</sup></a></span> Throughout his paper, Hodgson provided many additional details about the methods that Davey had used in the séances, including the choreography of the slate switching, the use of ‘invisible threads’ to seemingly animate bits of chalk, and the fact that Davey had used a small ‘thimble writer’ (a device designed secretly to affix to his finger, see <button class="ref show-table-fig-ref" data-id="f0006" data-behaviour="show-popup" data-popup-event-type="fig">Figure 6</button>) to surreptitiously mark the slates during his performances.
</p><div class="figureView"><div class="short-legend"><p class="captionText"><span aria-level="3" role="heading"><span class="captionLabel">Figure 6. </span></span> A secret thimble-writer gimmick of the type allegedly used by the medium Henry Slade. Hodgson ultimately revealed that Davey used a similar device as a means of secretly writing on slates during his simulated séances.<span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref en"><a data-rid="en0074" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="fn" data-label="footnote"><span class="off-screen">Footnote</span><sup>74</sup></a></span>.</p></div><a href="#" class="thumbnail" data-behaviour="show-popup" data-popup-event-type="fig" data-id="f0006"><img id="d1e581" src="/cms/asset/5cc84961-b62e-49c4-b999-39f7dbb812b6/ranr_a_2579758_f0006_b.gif" loading="lazy" height="240" width="500" alt="Figure 6. A secret thimble-writer gimmick of the type allegedly used by the medium Henry Slade. Hodgson ultimately revealed that Davey used a similar device as a means of secretly writing on slates during his simulated séances.Footnote74."></a><div><button class="show-full-size btn btn-sm" data-behaviour="show-popup" data-id="f0006" data-popup-event-type="fig">Display full size</button></div></div><p></p><p class="last">He also cited three key texts that contained instructions on how to perform the tricks that Davey had presented. The works referenced included Professor Hoffmann’s (a.k.a. Angelo John Lewis’s) <i>Modern Magic</i> and <i>More Magic</i>, Truesdell’s <i>The Bottom Facts Concerning the Science of Spiritualism</i>, and the anonymously authored <i>Revelations of a Spirit Medium</i>.<span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref en"><a data-rid="en0075" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="fn" data-label="footnote"><span class="off-screen">Footnote</span><sup>75</sup></a></span> Hodgson concluded, with a reference to Wallace’s previous accusation that Mr. Davey may have possessed genuine psychical powers, by writing that:
</p><div class="quote"><p class="first last">Now, statements like these, absurd as they seem to persons familiar with conjuring operations, do not originate simply and merely from ignorance of conjuring, and the remedy is not, simply and merely, to become acquainted with certain trick-devices. The remedy for such absurdity is to learn that an uninitiated witness cannot describe to himself the real conditions under which the feats were performed, because his powers of observation and memory are inadequate for the purpose. He may rightly conclude that under such and such conditions the feats are inexplicable by conjuring, but he wrongly concludes that the conditions were as he describes them.<span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref en"><a data-rid="en0076" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="fn" data-label="footnote"><span class="off-screen">Footnote</span><sup>76</sup></a></span></p></div><p></p></div></div><div class="NLM_sec NLM_sec_level_1" id="s0003"><h2 class="section-heading-2" id="d1e607">Connections to Subsequent Academic Research</h2><p>As experimental psychology developed as a formal academic discipline, researchers have made remarkable progress in refining our scientific understanding of the cognitive mechanisms that underpin human attention and memory. Much subsequent published research has served to reinforce even the most controversial findings reported by Hodgson and Davey back in 1887. Their results anticipate the formal emergence of several concepts, including inattentional blindness and reconstructive memory. And they also broadly pre-empt developments related to the discipline of anomalistic psychology and modern psychological interest in the science of magic. This is despite the fact that the Mal-Observation Report itself has been largely unattended and forgotten by later researchers.</p><div class="NLM_sec NLM_sec_level_2" id="s0003-s2001"><h3 class="section-heading-3" id="d1e610">Inattentional Blindness</h3><p>For readers unfamiliar with the term ‘inattentional blindness’ and who may not have seen a demonstration of the now classic basketball game paradigm, I highly recommend that you take a moment to watch the short (1 minute and 22 seconds) video before reading on. The clip is publicly available online (<a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=vJG698U2Mvo" target="_blank">https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=vJG698U2Mvo</a>), having been uploaded on YouTube by one of the original experimenters, Daniel Simons, under the title ‘Selective Attention Test’ on 10 March 2010 (see footnote for an archived stable link).<span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref en"><a data-rid="en0077" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="fn" data-label="footnote"><span class="off-screen">Footnote</span><sup>77</sup></a></span> The experiment from which the clip originates will be described in detail below, but actually watching the clip is one of the best ways of understanding the phenomena of inattentional blindness.</p><p>The concept of inattentional blindness was not formally established in psychological literature until the 1990s, when psychologists Irving Mack and Arian Rock published a book containing descriptions of a landmark series of experiments about how participants could fail to notice seemingly obvious stimuli that were fully visible and in plain sight when their attention was otherwise engaged.<span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref en"><a data-rid="en0078" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="fn" data-label="footnote"><span class="off-screen">Footnote</span><sup>78</sup></a></span> The book describes dozens of experiments that used simple computerised displays on monitors, conducted over the course of seven years, and involving hundreds of participants. In each experiment, participants would be explicitly instructed to engage in a simple ‘primary task’; for example, a cross-arm discrimination task (see <button class="ref show-table-fig-ref" data-id="f0007" data-behaviour="show-popup" data-popup-event-type="fig">Figure 7</button>), where they viewed a brief presentation of cross and were asked to indicate whether the horizontal or vertical lines were longer.
