</p><div class="figureView"><div class="short-legend"><p class="captionText"><span aria-level="3" role="heading"><span class="captionLabel">Figure 7. </span></span> Schematic of Mack and Rock’s computerized ‘cross-arm discrimination’ task.<span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref en"><a data-rid="en0079" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="fn" data-label="footnote"><span class="off-screen">Footnote</span><sup>79</sup></a></span> the top row represents a non-critical trial, where participants were instructed indicate whether the vertical or horizontal arms of the cross were longer. The second row represents a ‘critical trial’, participants received the same instructions, but in this case, an unexpected stimulus, a simple geometric shape, appeared alongside the cross. Participants were considered to be ‘inattentionally blind’ to the unexpected stimulus if they failed to report seeing it upon being questioned as to whether they noticed anything in addition to the cross being visible on the computer monitor.</p></div><a href="#" class="thumbnail" data-behaviour="show-popup" data-popup-event-type="fig" data-id="f0007"><img id="d1e637" src="/cms/asset/86733338-78b1-4252-81c2-4c7d8a0bbde5/ranr_a_2579758_f0007_b.gif" loading="lazy" height="377" width="500" alt="Figure 7. Schematic of Mack and Rock’s computerized ‘cross-arm discrimination’ task.Footnote79 the top row represents a non-critical trial, where participants were instructed indicate whether the vertical or horizontal arms of the cross were longer. The second row represents a ‘critical trial’, participants received the same instructions, but in this case, an unexpected stimulus, a simple geometric shape, appeared alongside the cross. Participants were considered to be ‘inattentionally blind’ to the unexpected stimulus if they failed to report seeing it upon being questioned as to whether they noticed anything in addition to the cross being visible on the computer monitor."></a><div><button class="show-full-size btn btn-sm" data-behaviour="show-popup" data-id="f0007" data-popup-event-type="fig">Display full size</button></div></div><p></p><p class="last">Unbeknown to participants, on certain trials an additional critical stimulus was introduced that appeared alongside the primary task stimuli on screen. The key measure of these studies was whether participants would be able to report any awareness of this critical stimulus when questioned immediately after the trial. In many cases, the participants’ responses indicated that they were entirely unaware of the critical stimulus, such participants were considered to be inattentionally blind. No only did Mack and Rock’s series of experiments provide firm evidence that people can fail to detect stimuli that are plainly visible when their attention is otherwise engaged, but their methodology also provided a conceptual framework for investigating and describing these failures of visual awareness.<span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref en"><a data-rid="en0080" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="fn" data-label="footnote"><span class="off-screen">Footnote</span><sup>80</sup></a></span> This framework set the stage for what is perhaps the most dramatic empirical examples of inattentional blindness in scientific literature: Simons and Chabris’ Invisible Gorilla paradigm.<span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref en"><a data-rid="en0081" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="fn" data-label="footnote"><span class="off-screen">Footnote</span><sup>81</sup></a></span> This experiment adapted Mack and Rock’s framework and applied it to a more naturalistic and dynamic stimulus set. Rather than using simple static geometric shapes that would appear on a computer monitor, their experiment involved showing participants a live-action video recording. The stimulus video depicts two ‘teams’ of players, one team is dressed in white shirts, and the other in black shirts. Each team has a basketball that they pass only to their respective teammates. For their primary task, rather than discriminating which arm of a cross was longer, participants were instructed to count the number of times players passed a basketball. The ‘game’ lasts for a total of 24 seconds, and the white team makes a total of 15 passes. The critical stimulus, rather than being a simple shape on the monitor, was a woman wearing a full-body gorilla costume, who appears on screen about 10 seconds into the video. She enters from the right side of the video frame, strolls amongst the other players, pauses briefly in the centre of the action, beats her chest, and then continues strolling off the screen. The woman in the gorilla costume is fully visible for 10 seconds. Nonetheless, when people are focusing on the basketball passes, over half of them fail to notice the gorilla. Inattentional blindness is a robustly replicable phenomenon. Multiple studies have demonstrated that this phenomenon can apply to various stimuli including simple geometric shapes, to airplanes, motorcycles, unicycling clowns, fire extinguishers, simulated assaults, and weapons.<span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref en"><a data-rid="en0082" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="fn" data-label="footnote"><span class="off-screen">Footnote</span><sup>82</sup></a></span> But it is also deeply counterintuitive.<span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref en"><a data-rid="en0083" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="fn" data-label="footnote"><span class="off-screen">Footnote</span><sup>83</sup></a></span> In surveys of the general population, the majority of respondents tend to endorse such statements as: ‘Human memory works like a video camera, accurately recording the events we see and hear so that we can review and inspect them later’ and that ‘People generally notice when something unexpected enters their field of view, even when they’re paying attention to something else’.<span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref en"><a data-rid="en0084" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="fn" data-label="footnote"><span class="off-screen">Footnote</span><sup>84</sup></a></span> Inattentional blindness studies reveal that the truth is significantly weirder than people tend to imagine. For Hodgson, watching other observers unknowingly experience inattentional blindness was one of the most fantastic elements of Davey’s performances. Hodgson attended five of the seventeen of the documented performances as an observer, and he wrote that ‘none of the sitters witnessed that best phenomenon, <i>Mr. Davey writing’</i>.<span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref en"><a data-rid="en0085" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="fn" data-label="footnote"><span class="off-screen">Footnote</span><sup>85</sup></a></span> In other words, Hodgson considered the best trick to be the fact that Davey was able to invisibly deploy many of his methods of writing the slates while in plain view of the sitters, who believed they were keenly observing him to detect trickery. Because Hodgson was expecting the trick, he was able to watch Davey openly executing his moves while the sitters failed to notice them. Today, narrative descriptions of inattentional blindness research usually begin with Mack &amp; Rock’s seminal experiments. Occasionally, Ulric Neisser’s original (although largely unpublished) work on ‘selective looking’ will be mentioned.<span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref en"><a data-rid="en0086" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="fn" data-label="footnote"><span class="off-screen">Footnote</span><sup>86</sup></a></span> But I propose that some of the best and earliest experimental evidence for the phenomena can be found, largely unnoticed, in the Mal-Observation Report, which was published more than 100 years before the concept of inattentional blindness gained mainstream scientific acceptance.</p></div><div class="NLM_sec NLM_sec_level_2" id="s0003-s2002"><h3 class="section-heading-3" id="d1e667">Reconstructive Memory and the Unreliability of Eyewitness Testimony</h3><p class="last">There is now a rich psychological literature on the potential unreliability of eyewitness testimony. The concept of ‘reconstructive memory’ is now widely accepted by the scientific community. The term refers to the idea that our memories are not recordings to be played back as much as narratives that we ‘reconstruct’ each time we engage in recall; as a consequence, what we perceive as memories are not only traces of past events, but are also composed of our expectations, imaginations, and assumptions.<span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref en"><a data-rid="en0087" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="fn" data-label="footnote"><span class="off-screen">Footnote</span><sup>87</sup></a></span> Once again, this is a deeply counterintuitive concept; the process of reconstruction is very different from the generally accepted idea that memory operates analogously to recording devices like video cameras. Evidence of false memories provides significant empirical support for the theoretical framework of reconstructive memory, and researchers have now repeatedly demonstrated that people are capable of confusing imaginary events with real memories.<span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref en"><a data-rid="en0088" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="fn" data-label="footnote"><span class="off-screen">Footnote</span><sup>88</sup></a></span> A key breakthrough in this literature can be traced to research conducted in the 1970s on how leading questions can cause witnesses to mistakenly report having seen imaginary events. Loftus and Palmer demonstrated that participants could be induced to remember seeing events that were not presented in response to leading questions.<span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref en"><a data-rid="en0089" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="fn" data-label="footnote"><span class="off-screen">Footnote</span><sup>89</sup></a></span> One week after having watched a video of a car accident, participants were explicitly asked, ‘Did you see any broken glass?’ Because the video itself did not actually show any broken glass, reported false memories of broken glass were arguably induced by the question itself. These results are particularly important because they demonstrate to participants that leading questions do not merely have the potential to change people’s <i>interpretations</i> of their own recollections; they can reliably cause people to create false memories of entirely fictional events. Subsequent studies have demonstrated that false verbal suggestions presented <i>cocurrently</i> with events can also induce false reports.<span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref en"><a data-rid="en0090" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="fn" data-label="footnote"><span class="off-screen">Footnote</span><sup>90</sup></a></span> While, researchers continue to explore and debate many details, the theory of reconstructive memory is now widely accepted. But, as with inattentional blindness, conventional narratives around the research do not extend back into the 1880s, rather, they typically begin with the work of Fredrick Bartlett, who not only drew a clear distinction between reproductive and reconstructive memory, but also argued that reconstructive memory processes were more typical of how people remember events in real-world circumstances. <span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref en"><a data-rid="en0091" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="fn" data-label="footnote"><span class="off-screen">Footnote</span><sup>91</sup></a></span> While the Mal-Observation Report did not directly inspire twentieth-century psychologists, it nonetheless can be seen on of the earliest examples evidence of reconstructive memory processes, and modern anomalistic psychologists now argue that memory illusions play an important role in accounts of paranormal experiences.<span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref en"><a data-rid="en0092" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="fn" data-label="footnote"><span class="off-screen">Footnote</span><sup>92</sup></a></span></p></div><div class="NLM_sec NLM_sec_level_2" id="s0003-s2003"><h3 class="section-heading-3" id="d1e695">‘The Science of Magic’ And Anomalistic Psychology</h3><p>Empirical investigations involving performance magic and illusion played a critical role in the establishment of Experimental Psychology as a scientific discipline, and several prominent early psychologists wrote about the psychology of magic tricks, particularly as they related to illusions of spiritualist phenomena.<span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref en"><a data-rid="en0093" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="fn" data-label="footnote"><span class="off-screen">Footnote</span><sup>93</sup></a></span> Most of these early writings represent early examples of a subdiscipline now known as anomalistic psychology. This is a branch of psychology that actively seeks naturalistic explanations for why many people report having extraordinary experiences that seem to be paranormal, i.e., outside the realm of conventional science. Christopher French has written that anomalistic psychology ‘attempts to explain paranormal and related beliefs and ostensibly paranormal experiences in terms of known (or knowable) psychological or physical factors’.<span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref en"><a data-rid="en0094" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="fn" data-label="footnote"><span class="off-screen">Footnote</span><sup>94</sup></a></span> The Mal-Observation Report represents an excellent early example of this principle, in that the researchers deliberately used naturalistic methods (i.e., conjuring/magic tricks) to help develop a better psychological understanding of how people might come to report extraordinary paranormal experiences. Notably, the power of these deceptions to falsely induce paranormal experiences has not diminished with time: In 1980, Singer and Benassi used magic trick methods to convince university students that they had witnessed a genuine demonstration of psychokinesis (aka metal bending).<span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref en"><a data-rid="en0095" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="fn" data-label="footnote"><span class="off-screen">Footnote</span><sup>95</sup></a></span> Lesaffre and colleagues, showed that contemporary undergraduate students still reliably endorsed mediumistic explanations in response to magic performances.<span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref en"><a data-rid="en0096" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="fn" data-label="footnote"><span class="off-screen">Footnote</span><sup>96</sup></a></span> Other researchers have adapted some of the same methods used by Davey to create convincing illusions of extraordinary technology, convincing participants dummy equipment is variously capable of reading their minds or controlling their thoughts; instead of pretending that the source of the extraordinary phenomena is attributable to spirits, these researchers gesture towards (fictional) neuroscientific tech and algorithms.<span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref en"><a data-rid="en0097" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="fn" data-label="footnote"><span class="off-screen">Footnote</span><sup>97</sup></a></span> These continued investigations promise to help shed new light on how people can formulate and update their beliefs in response to unusual experiences. Looking back on the Mal-Observation Report through the lens of contemporary research in anomalistic psychology, an important takeaway is how relatively minor errors of perception and memory, combined with a bit of deliberate deception and a pre-existing will to believe, can create a cascade of reasoning errors that can result in the formation of extraordinary beliefs.</p><p class="last">Performance magic was largely ignored by the scientific community throughout the twentieth century.<span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref en"><a data-rid="en0098" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="fn" data-label="footnote"><span class="off-screen">Footnote</span><sup>98</sup></a></span> There is no definitive answer as to why twentieth<sup>−</sup>century psychologists largely disengaged with magic as a research tool; however, two potential explanations include early psychologists’ motivations to actively distance themselves from what they viewed as the pseudoscientific associations of psychical research and the prominence of behaviourism.<span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref en"><a data-rid="en0099" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="fn" data-label="footnote"><span class="off-screen">Footnote</span><sup>99</sup></a></span> Both explanations stem from researchers attempting to more firmly establish psychology as a formally recognised science. In the case of psychical research, this meant establishing clear boundaries between scientific psychology and psychical research, and magic tricks were arguably too closely associated with mediumistic fraud for researchers to be comfortable engaging with them professionally. Similarly, behaviourism, with its rejection of any type of psychological data that could not be reduced to an externally measurable behaviour, effectively rules out the study of magic and illusion, given that these experiences only occur as internal representations in people’s minds. However, throughout the past 25 years, researchers have increasingly been turning to magic trick methods as a tool for developing new paradigms to explore human cognition.<span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref en"><a data-rid="en0100" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="fn" data-label="footnote"><span class="off-screen">Footnote</span><sup>100</sup></a></span> Performance magic represents a rich, and still relatively underutilised, resource for cognitive scientists.<span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref en"><a data-rid="en0101" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="fn" data-label="footnote"><span class="off-screen">Footnote</span><sup>101</sup></a></span> The idea of adapting magic trick methods to psychology experiments has proven to be productive, with researchers developing new laboratory paradigms that help illuminate a wide variety of cognitive mechanisms, including inattentional blindness, change blindness, introspection, decision making, problem solving, memory, and amodal perception.<span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref en"><a data-rid="en0102" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="fn" data-label="footnote"><span class="off-screen">Footnote</span><sup>102</sup></a></span> Although neither Hodgson nor Davey identified themselves as psychologists, The Mal-Observation Report arguably established the first-ever framework for how to adapt magic trick methods to aid in psychological research.</p></div></div><div class="NLM_sec NLM_sec_level_1" id="s0004"><h2 class="section-heading-2" id="d1e734">Conclusion</h2><p class="last">Throughout their writing, Hodgson and Davey were careful to acknowledge that just because they could <i>simulate</i> mediumistic phenomena using magic trick methods, it did not necessarily follow that every testimony related to a spiritualistic experience was attributable to deception. Both men left open the possibility that scientific advancements and new experimental work might one day vindicate some of the empirical claims of proponents of Modern Spiritualism.<span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref en"><a data-rid="en0103" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="fn" data-label="footnote"><span class="off-screen">Footnote</span><sup>103</sup></a></span> To date, while discussions do continue, the mainstream scientific establishment has not come to embrace the principles of modern spiritualism in ways that were prophesized by writers like Alfred Russell Wallace and Arthur Conan Doyle.<span class="ref-lnk lazy-ref en"><a data-rid="en0104" href="#" data-behaviour="toggle-ref" data-ref-type="fn" data-label="footnote"><span class="off-screen">Footnote</span><sup>104</sup></a></span> Nonetheless, the Mal-Observation Report represents a valuable contribution to psychological research. Many of the report’s most controversial findings about testimony, deception, attention, memory, and anomalous experience have since been borne out by subsequent formal empirical investigations related to inattentional blindness, reconstructive memory, anomalistic psychology, and the science of magic. Even though we arguably cannot contact the dead in a mediumistic sense, Hodgson and Davey’s voices live on through their writings, and can still provide valuable lessons to for contemporary readers.</p></div><div class="pb-dropzone" data-pb-dropzone="scholixLinksDropZone"><!-- Empty dropzone --></div><div id="ack" class="NLM_sec"><h3 class="section-heading-3" id="d1e1319">Acknowledgments</h3><p class="last">The ideas for this article were catalysed by some excellent discussions at both the Science of Magic Association Conference and Occultism and Populare Culture Conference. Special thanks also to the folks who maintain the Society for Psychical Research archive as well as The International Association for the Preservation of Spiritualist and Occult Periodicals archive.</p></div><div id="coi-statement" class="NLM_sec"><h2 class="section-heading-2" id="d1e1322">Disclosure Statement</h2><p class="last">No potential conflict of interest was reported by the author(s).</p></div></div><script nonce="9fb3e83d3eecd7dd-SJC">tandf.tfviewerdata={"table-index-map":{},"tables":[],"figures":[{"content":"<div class=\"figure-content-f0001\"><div class=\"paragraph\"><caption><p class=\"captionText\"><span aria-level=\"3\" role=\"heading\"><span class=\"captionLabel\">Figure 1. <\/span><\/span> Example of an ‘ungimmicked’ school slate. The second row represents a ‘critical trial’, participants received the same instructions, but in this case, an unexpected stimulus, a simple geometric shape, appeared alongside the cross. Participants were considered to be ‘inattentionally blind’ to the unexpected stimulus if they failed to report seeing it upon being questioned as to whether they noticed anything in addition to the cross being visible on the computer monitor.<\/p><\/caption><\/div><img src=\"\/cms\/asset\/1719c58b-991f-4572-a4cf-0e744e5ce881\/ranr_a_2579758_f0007_b.jpg\" loading=\"lazy\" alt=\"Figure 7. Fysiografiska Sällskapet i Lund.</div></div><h2 id="inline_frontnotes">Notes</h2><div class="summation-section"><div id="en0001"><p class="first last">1. Mack and Rock’s book, <i>Inattentional Blindness</i>, was published in 1998 and is largely responsible for popularising the term. However, the term inattentional blindness originally appeared in print in 1992, when researchers noted that several of their participants failed to detect plainly presented visual stimuli when their attention was otherwise engaged: “One striking and unexpected finding is that without attention many subjects had no awareness of the stimulus object, an effect we call inattentional blindness.”</p><p class="last">See Rock et al., “Perception without Attention,” 502–534; and Mack and Rock, <i>Inattentional Blindness</i>.</p></div><div id="en0006"><p class="first last">6. This date/location (1883/Leipzig) for the “First Experimental Psychology Laboratory” is somewhat debatable, but 1883 <i>does</i> mark the lab where (a) experimental psychology research was conducted that was (b) officially sanctioned by a university. William James had established a teaching laboratory at Harvard in 1875, but the lab was exclusively for teaching and demonstration purposes <i>not</i> experimental research. And Wundt was conducting experiments as early as 1879, but on an ad hoc basis that was not officially sanctioned by the University of Leipzig. See Cattell, “Early Psychological Laboratories,”1928; Bringmann, Bringmann, and Ungerer, “Wundt’s Laboratory,” 1980; and ’ Marshall and Wendt, “Wilhelm Wundt,” 1980.</p></div><div id="en0024"><p class="first last">24. Experiences,” 659–669; Wilson, and French, “Magic and Memory”; and Beth, and Ekroll, “The Curious Influence,” 513–522; and Tompkins et al., “Phantom Vanish.”</p></div><div id="en0103"><p class="first">103. Towards the end of his life, Richard Hodgson did actually endorse a medium, Leonora Piper, as possessing genuine mediumistic abilities. He believed that her abilities provided empirical proof for the hypothesis that consciousness survives after bodily death. Although, many sceptics never fully embraced his evidence and arguments. Somewhat surreally, the most thoroughly documented account of Hodgson’s life is a biography published in 1949 by Alex Baird. A non-spiritualist reader might be tempted to interpret some elements of Hodgson’s conversion to spiritualism and subsequent eccentricities through a lens of psychiatric disorder. But Baird, an unapologetic believer in the survival of the soul after death, celebrates Hodgson’s conversion as a true revelation and a positive personal development. In fact, his biography does not conclude with Hodgson’s physical death, but instead it continues with accounts of subsequent communications allegedly originating from Hodgson’s spirit.</p><p class="last">See Richard Hodgson, “Certain Phenomena of Trance,” 284–582; Gardner, “Piper Bamboozled William James,” 252–62. Baird, <i>Hodgson;</i> and Morton Prince, ‘Prince to Putnam, 322–327.</p></div><div id="en0104"><p class="first last">104. Cardeña, “Parapsychological Phenomena,” 663; Reber and Alcock, “Parapsychology’s Elusive Quest,” 391; Doyle, <i>History</i>; and Wallace, <i>Miracles.</i></p></div></div><div class="pb-dropzone no-border-top" data-pb-dropzone="contentNavigationDropZoneFull"><div class="widget gql-content-navigation none  widget-none" id="d59557d1-e1fb-43ae-9542-192f8d2a53a7">
